\title{Thinking in Pretraining:\\ Eighty Runs of Negative Results, and the Instruments That Produced Them}
\author{}
\date{\today}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We ask whether a language model can learn, during pretraining and without supervision of content, to write a short thought that makes the text it is about to predict more likely. A 0.6B model trained from scratch emits a free-form thought at fixed anchors; the thought is spliced at overlapping integer positions and the next $k$ real tokens are scored with it and without it in the same forward pass, so a thought's value is a measured counterfactual in nats. Ninety-eight runs later, the answer at this scale is still no, and the interesting content of this paper is \emph{why}, measured rather than argued. Four results carry it. (i)~\textbf{The reader is not the bottleneck}: a true $12$-token hint drawn from inside the scored window is worth $+4$ to $+11$ nats, the same tokens shuffled are worth $0$, and a hint drawn from beyond the window---the target we supervised for three billion tokens---is worth $-1.6$. The reader is also \emph{selective} in a way that forecloses the obvious escapes: hide $32$ context tokens from it and hand the thought slot the exact text it is missing, and it recovers $-0.27 \pm 0.18$ of the ${\approx}8$ nats that hiding cost, while the true future tokens are worth $+9.85 \pm 0.59$ under the same mask---the slot's only currency is text shaped like the continuation, not information as such. (ii)~\textbf{Nothing the model can produce at inference beats an empty slot}: against content-free filler on the same weights, its own thought loses at every length from $2$ to $12$ tokens, on seven checkpoints under four different reward shapes---by $0.1$ to $0.9$ nats depending on the run and the length, and by $0.81 \pm 0.08$ at the widest of the seven (run 34 @ $800$), with the same ordering intact at the untrained initialisation ($-0.570 \pm 0.139$ against length-matched filler). On run 98's final checkpoint (step $5{,}320$; Section~\ref{sec:negative}) the two are instead indistinguishable at $k{=}1$ ($+0.005 \pm 0.017$ nats, $384$ anchors), both $0.22$ nats below silence---though at $k{=}16$ the written thought is still $0.071 \pm 0.035$ behind the empty slot: by the end the cost of thinking is the slot and not what is written in it. (iii)~\textbf{The writer can forecast but not free-running}: with the true prefix teacher-forced into the slot it predicts the next true token $30\%$ of the time against a $41\%$ clean-text ceiling, and $6\%$ when it writes the prefix itself---the regime every run trained in was never the regime any run samples in. (iv)~\textbf{Reader miscalibration was real and is fixable, and it is not enough}: training the reader only on one unselected thought of the model's own halved the cost of thinking ($-2.45 \to -1.17$ nats) and then saturated, with order-borne value never once positive. We also report the mistakes that these instruments caught in our own work---bf16 parameters that froze half the network, a learning rate that destroyed the base, a supervised target the reader values below silence, an offset lottery that made the target unlearnable by construction, and thought positions one step out of register, worth $0.85$ nats by themselves---because each was invisible in the training curves and each cost days. On the last checkpoint we put through the zero-shot suite---run 34's step $800$, the one benchmarked checkpoint that writes words into the slot---the suite agrees with the held-out probes and adds the plainest artefact we have: a thought at inference costs $1.4$ macro points against the same weights in clean mode ($p = 4\times10^{-4}$ over $4{,}000$ paired examples), and the thought being scored is $258$ distinct strings across those $4{,}000$ examples---one of them emitted at $1{,}083$ of them. What the writer learned is a register, not a forecast. The design, the probes and the falsification record are the contribution; the mechanism is not yet one. Three late results qualify this record. With the reader frozen at initialisation, so that every baseline is an exact reference, training buys \emph{content}---a note worth $+2.79 \pm 0.76$ nats more for its own anchor than for its neighbour's, where the untrained model's is worth zero---but not \emph{utility}: under that reader a context-free row of digits beats every writer we trained, and reinforcement on the plain reward converges to it, including a writer that is shown the answer (Section~\ref{sec:frozen}). The last seventeen runs (82--98) test~(i) directly, by splicing text we choose into the thought slot of an archived checkpoint and scoring the window with no update: a correct $16$-token thought is worth $+5.3$ to $+9.7$ nats at six of the seven checkpoints measured, including the untrained initialisation all of them start from, so the reader's willingness to pay is not something any of these designs created and not something any of them destroyed; at the final design's own deployment anchors the model's own thought is worth about $-0.22$ nats per thought, flat over the programme's last eight checkpoints, and yet $39\%$ of those thoughts \emph{help} by $+0.48$ each, so the same writer under a perfect speak-or-stay-silent gate would be worth $+0.19$---a selection ceiling no label-free gate we tried reaches, and one that priced per real token \emph{at the same corpus mixture the damage is measured on} ($+0.0022$, or $+0.0143$ with oracle selection among six samples at four times the trigger volume) is still below the $+0.041$ nats per token of base damage the run paid to obtain it, in every one of the three held-out domains taken separately; and asked to draft the next $8$ tokens and be scored on exactly those tokens, the trained writer reproduces $2.3\% \pm 0.6\%$ of them against the untrained initialisation's $2.4\% \pm 0.6\%$---training moved what a draft \emph{costs} and left what it \emph{says} where it was (Section~\ref{sec:negative}); and the last design question we had---change \emph{where} the model thinks rather than what it is rewarded for---is answered negatively without any training, because the positions a longer-trained model learns are uncorrelated with the positions where the model's own thought helps ($\rho = +0.002 \pm 0.028$ over $1{,}522$ anchors) and the most learnable of them are the worst. And an implementation error left the $64$ data-parallel replicas unsynchronised from run 13 onwards, so every learning outcome after it was obtained at one sixty-fourth of the stated batch; we flag which conclusions that can change.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}

Standard pretraining gives a model one forward pass per token. Some tokens are easy and waste it; some sit at the end of a chain of implicit reasoning and exceed it. The idea we test is the obvious one: let the model write a few ordinary vocabulary tokens first---a \emph{thought}---and then predict the real text with that thought in context. Nothing supervises what a thought should say. It is credited only by what it does: the discounted log-likelihood of the true continuation with the thought, minus the same quantity without it, computed in the same forward pass so the baseline is free and exact.

The appeal is that this is unsupervised, needs no task data, and asks the model for the one thing next-token prediction never asks: commit to something about how the text will develop, before seeing it. The difficulty, which we did not appreciate for many runs, is that a thought is a function of the context, so it cannot carry information the context lacks; it can only pay by (a) being a correct forecast the reader can exploit, or (b) buying serial computation. We now have measurements of both channels, and both are closed at this scale: the forecast is too rarely correct to be worth its confident errors, and the computation channel---measured with content-free filler in the same slots---is worth approximately zero in the geometry where we first measured it, is squarely negative at the high-entropy anchors the last designs deploy at ($-0.23$ nats for six tokens on run 98 @ $5{,}320$, the final checkpoint), and is never worth less than what the model writes in its place: at best, by the end, exactly as much.

This paper is therefore a negative-results paper with a positive methodological core. The core is a set of cheap, zero-update probes that answer questions a training curve cannot: where in the future the value of a hint lives; what the model's own thought is worth at inference with nothing selected; what an \emph{empty} slot is worth in the same place; how much of a thought the model gets right, teacher-forced versus free-running; and whether the slot's geometry is costing anything. Every claim below is one of those measurements, on held-out text, with the number the model would actually get---never a best-of-$R$ winner, which inflates by construction.

\paragraph{Contributions.}
(1)~A precise counterfactual objective for thinking during pretraining, and a training procedure (mixed supervised/reinforcement anchors in one sequence, compulsory thinking, $R$ rollouts with paired baselines) that is stable, never collapses the base, and costs about $4\times$ vanilla per token.
(2)~Ten instruments (Section~\ref{sec:instruments}) that localise the failure, including the \emph{filler floor}: the value of extra positions with no content, which we argue should be the reporting bar for any work of this kind, and which no checkpoint of ours has cleared---the last one (run 98 @ $5{,}320$) reaches it at $k{=}1$ and does not pass it at any horizon.
(3)~A falsification record: seven negative results (Section~\ref{sec:negative}); the first five are each replicated across reward shapes, supervision fractions, thought lengths and base maturities, the sixth is measured directly on archived checkpoints of four designs and the untrained rung they share, and the seventh asks whether the failure is an artefact of the positions we chose and answers no, with no training at all.
(4)~A catalogue of our own mistakes and the measurement that caught each (Section~\ref{sec:mistakes}), including a numerical-precision failure that silently freezes half of a network's weights under plain AdamW.
(5)~The conditions under which we think the idea can still work, stated as quantities rather than hopes (Section~\ref{sec:whatwouldwork}).
(6)~A cheap decision rule for designs of this kind: both absolute arms of the splicing probe, about two minutes of CPU each, read at a run's \emph{first} archived checkpoint $190$ steps in, which is where essentially all of the movement a design produces in its reader already is---three of the runs in this paper could have been decided that way instead of after a day of training.

\section{Method}
\label{sec:method}

\subsection{Setup and notation}

Let $t_{1:T}$ be a training sequence ($T=1024$; $2048$ for the longer-thought geometry). \emph{Anchors} sit at a fixed stride: one every $k$ tokens from position $64$, so that reward windows tile the sequence and never overlap ($N=14$ anchors at $k=64$). In the first two phases of the programme placement is therefore capacity, not policy; Section~\ref{sec:budget} describes how the model takes over placement under a budget in the third. Earlier designs placed anchors by an entropy trigger with a context floor (Appendix~\ref{app:previous}); the fixed stride replaced them because it makes every mask, position and row index a per-run constant, and one compiled graph serves every step.

At an anchor $n$, the model generates a thought $\thought = (\thought_1, \dots, \thought_L)$ of $L$ ordinary vocabulary tokens. Write $\ctx = t_{1:n}$ for the context and $\win = t_{n+1:n+k}$ for the next $k$ true tokens, the \emph{reward window}. One set of parameters $\theta$ plays two roles:
\begin{align}
    \text{the \emph{writer}:} \quad & \policy(\thought \mid \ctx) = \prod_{i=1}^{L} \policy(\thought_i \mid \ctx, \thought_{<i}), \\
    \text{the \emph{reader}:} \quad & \reader(\win \mid \ctx, \thought) = \prod_{j=1}^{k} \reader(t_{n+j} \mid \ctx, \thought, t_{n+1:n+j-1}).
\end{align}
The writer produces the thought; the reader consumes it; both are the same transformer. In the implementation, thoughts live in a spliced buffer appended to the sequence: window tokens attend to their thought through an additive attention mask, all other real tokens mask every thought out, and thought blocks never see each other. Thoughts are therefore \emph{ephemeral}: only the $k$ tokens of the reward window read the thought, which keeps windows independent and keeps a bad thought from contaminating the rest of the sequence.

Generation is deliberately unconstrained (\emph{free-form}): the writer samples from the full vocabulary with top-$512$ truncation, temperature $1.0$ and a frequency penalty. We impose no vocabulary allowlist and no format. The closing token \tend{} is an ordinary action: a thought may stop after $L_{\min}$ tokens and must stop at $L$.

\subsection{Objective}

The ideal objective at an anchor is the marginal likelihood of the true window,
\begin{equation}
    \log \reader(\win \mid \ctx) = \log \sum_{\thought} \policy(\thought \mid \ctx)\, \reader(\win \mid \ctx, \thought),
\end{equation}
whose intractable sum we lower-bound by sampling thoughts from the writer:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{J}(\theta) = \E_{\thought \sim \policy(\cdot \mid \ctx)} \big[ f_\theta(\thought) \big],
    \qquad
    f_\theta(\thought) := \log \reader(\win \mid \ctx, \thought).
    \label{eq:objective}
\end{equation}
The bound is tight when the writer equals the posterior $\reader(\thought \mid \ctx, \win)$: the distribution over thoughts given knowledge of how the text continues. The writer conditions on the past only, so training squeezes it toward the best past-conditioned approximation of a future-aware posterior. This gap is the definition of useful thinking: the model must predict which deliberation will pay off before it knows the answer.

\subsection{Long-horizon discounted gain}
\label{sec:gain}

The scalar that drives all learning about thought content is the \emph{discounted counterfactual gain}
\begin{equation}
    \gain(\thought) \;=\; \sum_{j=1}^{k} \gamma^{\,j-1} \Big[ \log \reader\big(t_{n+j} \mid \ctx, \thought, t_{n+1:n+j-1}\big) \;-\; \log \reader\big(t_{n+j} \mid \ctx, t_{n+1:n+j-1}\big) \Big],
    \label{eq:gain}
\end{equation}
the improvement the thought causes in the log-likelihood of the true window, term by term, relative to the same model without the thought. The no-thought pass is computed anyway for ordinary NTP, so the counterfactual baseline is free, and it removes the intrinsic difficulty of the position: a hard window has low log-likelihood with or without the thought, and $\gain$ isolates the causal contribution. We use $k = 64$ and $\gamma = 0.97$ ($k=128$ for the longer-thought geometry), giving an effective horizon $\sum_{j=1}^{k}\gamma^{j-1} = (1-\gamma^{k})/(1-\gamma) \approx 28.6$ tokens: credit concentrates where the thought's causal influence is strongest while still extending far beyond the thought itself.

\paragraph{Two losses, two questions.}
Equation~\eqref{eq:gain} is what separates this objective from NTP, so it is worth stating what each measures. The NTP loss scores the local surface: the next token given the visible past. The gain scores \emph{anticipation}: it is nonzero only for content, committed to before the window is seen, that changes how well the model predicts the way the text develops over the effective horizon. A thought can therefore earn reward only through information about the \emph{evolution} of the text---the intermediate step of a derivation, the entity an argument is converging on, the structure of what follows---and never through fluency, plausibility, or local pattern completion, all of which the counterfactual subtraction cancels. In this precise sense the reader term trains prediction while the writer signal trains anticipation, and the two share one set of weights: the hypothesis under test is that representations shaped by the second question improve answers to the first.

\paragraph{Why the horizon must exceed the thought.}
Consider the myopic version of Eq.~\eqref{eq:gain} with $k \le L$ and no discount. The gain is maximized by any $\thought$ that makes $\win$ certain, and the verbatim copy $\thought = \win$ achieves this whenever the reader can learn to transcribe---an easy skill for an attention model. The optimum of the myopic objective is therefore \emph{copying, not thinking}, and Appendix~\ref{sec:failures} shows our $k=8$ runs discovered exactly this. With $k = 64 \gg L = 16$ the copy strategy cannot cover the window, and---more fundamentally---the writer does not know $\win$; a copy-like thought can only transcribe the model's own best guess, which the next mechanism prices at zero.

\subsection{The training procedure}
\label{sec:procedure}

Every design in Appendix~\ref{app:superseded} shares one structural weakness, and eighteen runs were needed to see it. The model was always allowed to \emph{decline}. A learned gate decided whether to open a thought at all, and because a thought costs tokens and a bad thought costs likelihood, the cheapest available policy was to stop thinking: in every reinforcement run $p(\tstart)$ fell through many orders of magnitude to the exploration floor. Where the gate was held open, the same evasion reappeared one level down---the model made its thoughts \emph{inert}, so that $\gain$ and $\gain_{\mathrm{shuf}}$ rose together and their difference contracted to zero from either side. A thought that does nothing is never punished. The procedure below removes both escapes.

\paragraph{Anchors carry supervision and reinforcement in the same sequence.} Anchors are placed every $k$ tokens. Each is assigned, by coin, to one of two roles. A fraction $\rho = \tfrac14$ are \emph{supervised}: the thought slot is filled with the true continuation from beyond the scored window, teacher-forced at a jittered length, and the model is trained to produce it by ordinary cross-entropy. The remaining $\tfrac34$ are \emph{reinforcement} anchors: the model writes its own thought and is scored on what that thought does. Both kinds occupy identical \tstart{}\ldots\tend{} slots a few dozen tokens apart in one sequence. This matters more than it appears: in the previous designs (Appendix~\ref{app:previous}) the two channels lived in \emph{separate micro-batches}, so the model never saw a supervised thought and a self-written one in the same context, and ``write thoughts that look like the supervised ones'' had to travel through the weights across batches rather than being visible as a within-context pattern.

\paragraph{Thinking is compulsory.} There is no learned gate. At every reinforcement anchor the model must emit a thought of at least $L_{\min}$ tokens. An inert thought is therefore no longer free---it scores $\gain \approx 0$ and still pays its per-token price, which is strictly negative---so the policy cannot buy safety by saying nothing. Removing the gate costs an observable we valued (\emph{when} to think is no longer learned) and we regard that as the correct trade: a question the model answers by refusing to participate is not a question worth asking of it yet.

\paragraph{The reward is the raw counterfactual gain.} Runs 13$'$--17 rewarded the shuffle-corrected gain $\gain - \gain_{\mathrm{shuf}}$, which pays only for what token \emph{order} buys. That correction was introduced to close the register exploit (Appendix~\ref{sec:failures}), and it did---but it also made the reward almost unwinnable, because the order-borne component of an immature policy's samples is a small negative constant (Appendix~\ref{sec:run17}). With the supervised channel now holding the policy on the manifold of real future text at every step, the exploit it defended against is closed by construction, and the reward reverts to the quantity the objective actually names: did this thought make the true continuation more likely.

\paragraph{The shuffle survives as an instrument.} We continue to score a token-shuffled copy of each winning thought, at the cost of one forward pass, and it never enters the loss. Raw gain is winnable by topic priming---putting relevant vocabulary in context, which a bag of words does equally well---so without the minimal pair we could not distinguish priming from anticipation, and that distinction \emph{is} the scientific claim. Demoting the shuffle from reward to instrument is the single most consequential change here: it is what lets the objective be winnable while keeping the measurement honest.

\subsection{Search width: $R$ rollouts with paired-sample baselines}
\label{sec:rollouts}

A single sampled thought per anchor is the binding choice of the earlier designs: the audit prices genuinely informative thoughts correctly, at $+1.7$ nats, but an immature policy proposes one well under $1\%$ of the time (Appendix~\ref{sec:run13obs}), so the mean never leaves zero. We therefore draw $R$ rollouts $\thought^{(1)},\dots,\thought^{(R)}$ per reinforcement anchor (plus a greedy row, retained as a length diagnostic), score each once, and use them two ways.

\textbf{Paired-sample baselines} \citep{kool2019buy}. With $r_i = \gain(\thought^{(i)}) - \lambda L_i$ the raw gain of rollout $i$ net of its token price, the advantage is
\begin{equation}
A_i \;=\; r_i \;-\; \frac{1}{R-1}\sum_{j \neq i} r_j ,
\label{eq:paired}
\end{equation}
so each rollout is baselined by the mean of its siblings \emph{at the same anchor}. This is unbiased, costs no extra forward pass, and---unlike cross-anchor whitening---removes the anchor's intrinsic difficulty, so an easy position no longer makes all of its rollouts look good. By construction $\sum_i A_i = 0$ at every anchor. Advantages are then standardised across the reinforcement anchors of the micro-batch, clipped, and negative advantages are damped by a factor $4$ (a lesson of Appendix~\ref{sec:run13obs}: undamped negatives pull the writer toward silence faster than positives pull it toward anything).

\textbf{Best-of-$R$ selection.} The anchor's winner $\arg\max_i \gain(\thought^{(i)})$ is the content spliced into the training buffer, so the reader is trained on it and the policy gradient acts on it with the winner's own paired advantage. One token-shuffled copy of the winner is scored in a further pass; it enters no loss (Section~\ref{sec:procedure}) and is the instrument behind every claim about order-borne value below.

\textbf{A reporting discipline this forces.} Best-of-$R$ inflates the selected rollout's apparent value for selection reasons alone: on pure noise, $\E[\max_i - \operatorname{mean}_i]$ is $+0.57$ standard deviations at $R{=}2$ and $+1.42$ at $R{=}8$. We therefore report $\gain$ as the \emph{mean over rollouts}, the $R{=}1$-equivalent quantity that stays comparable across runs, and record the winner separately; $\gain_{\mathrm{shuf}}$ is measured on the winner. Two differences can then be formed and neither is unbiased: $\gain - \gain_{\mathrm{shuf}}$ compares the mean rollout with the winner's shuffle, two different thoughts, and is biased \emph{negative} because the winner's shuffle inherits the winner's topical advantage; $\gain_{\mathrm{win}} - \gain_{\mathrm{shuf}}$ compares the winner with its own shuffle and is biased \emph{upward}, because among $R$ rollouts the winner is selected partly for a lucky order. The unbiased instrument is the shuffle of a \emph{non-selected} rollout---what runs 16--17 scored for every rollout---at the cost of one further pass; the pilots below did not score it, and the programme does.

\subsection{The one gradient pass}
\label{sec:losses}

Differentiating Eq.~\eqref{eq:objective} gives
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_\theta \mathcal{J}
    = \underbrace{\E_{\thought}\big[ \nabla_\theta f_\theta(\thought) \big]}_{\text{reader term}}
    + \underbrace{\E_{\thought}\big[ f_\theta(\thought)\, \nabla_\theta \log \policy(\thought \mid \ctx) \big]}_{\text{writer term}}.
    \label{eq:gradient}
\end{equation}
Both terms, and the ordinary NTP loss, are computed in one forward--backward pass over the spliced buffer as three weighted cross-entropy sums over the same hidden states. \emph{Clean NTP} acts on every real token with weight $1$: the vanilla objective is unchanged. The \emph{reader term} acts on the window rows of every thinking anchor, discounted by $\gamma^{j-1}$ along the window, with weight $0.3$; it teaches the model to \emph{use} whatever sits in the thought slot, supervised or self-written. The \emph{writer term} acts on the thought rows: at supervised anchors it is plain cross-entropy on the true continuation (weight $1$, never decayed); at reinforcement anchors it is the score-function estimator with the winner's paired advantage of Eq.~\eqref{eq:paired} as a signed per-row weight (weight $0.15$, ramped in over the first $600$ steps and constant thereafter). The relative weights encode the position of Section~\ref{sec:role}: imitation is the teacher, reinforcement is the explorer, and neither is ever switched off.

\subsection{Splicing thoughts at integer positions}
\label{sec:splice}

Thought tokens need positions, and the choice is not cosmetic: our first scheme placed thoughts at fractional offsets inside $(n, n{+}1)$ and made perfect information worth $-1.3$ nats (Appendix~\ref{sec:positions}). The adopted scheme is \emph{integer splicing}: real tokens keep their standard positions $1,\dots,T$; the $i$-th token of a thought anchored at $n$ receives position $n + i$ (integers continuing from the anchor), overlapping the positions of the upcoming real tokens. The overlap is harmless---attention masks keep the streams separate, and rotary attention only sees relative offsets---while the thought sits at the natural distance from both its context and its window. Real-token geometry is untouched, so thinking remains positionally invisible to the main sequence.

Every evaluation is \emph{position-matched}: any comparison between thought variants (oracle, sampled, random, filler) places all variants at identical positions, so measured differences are differences of \emph{content}, never geometry. The oracle test is now run before and after every training phase as standard calibration.

\section{The instruments}
\label{sec:instruments}

Every failure in this project was invisible in the training loss and visible in one of the following measurements. All are zero-update, run on held-out text, and cost minutes to an hour of CPU beside the accelerator.

\paragraph{The counterfactual gain (in training, every step).} For each anchor, the discounted log-likelihood of the next $k$ true tokens with the thought minus the same without it, both read off one forward pass over the spliced buffer. We log it for an \emph{unselected} sampled rollout ($g_{r_0}$), for the greedy thought ($g_{\mathrm{spec}}$, what a user would get), for the best of $R$ (reported only as a selection diagnostic), and for a token-shuffled copy, whose difference from the unshuffled one is the order-borne value $\mathrm{ob}_0$.

\paragraph{The oracle-offset probe (\texttt{probe\_oracle\_offset.py}).} Splice a \emph{perfect} $12$-token hint taken from $o$ tokens ahead of the anchor and score the window. It answers where the value of a hint lives, and it is what showed that our supervised target lay outside that region entirely (Section~\ref{sec:offsetprobe}).

\paragraph{The spliced-arms probe (\texttt{agent/hint\_eval.py}).} The two-arm form of the same idea, and the instrument the last third of the programme rests on. At one fixed set of held-out anchors we splice text \emph{we} choose into the thought slot of an archived checkpoint and score the next $16$ true tokens with no update, under two arms: a \emph{verbatim} copy of the continuation, and a \emph{gold paraphrase} of it---true, non-verbatim, written by a 3B instruct model with the same leakage filter as the training hints, which is the target the hint runs were built around. We report the two arms as absolute levels and not as their difference, because a large difference between them can mean that one arm is useful or that both are catastrophic, and those two readings have nothing in common; one of our own mistakes (Section~\ref{sec:mistakes}) was almost reporting the second as the first.

\paragraph{The future-self ruler (two vanilla ladder rungs, per position).} Per-token cross-entropy of two \emph{vanilla} checkpoints of the same architecture on the same held-out sequences, differenced position by position: the $9$B-token rung every thinking run initialises from and the $12$B-token rung $11{,}400$ ordinary pre-training steps later. The difference is a cheap proxy for which positions are learnable at all at this scale, with no thought and no training anywhere in it, and it is what lets us ask whether the places a self-written thought helps are the places more pre-training helps (Section~\ref{sec:negative}, N7).

\paragraph{The think-mode probe (\texttt{probe\_thinkmode.py}).} The model writes its own thought at every anchor of a held-out sequence, sampled and greedy, nothing selected, and we report the change in window cross-entropy. This is the headline number of the project and the only one that corresponds to using the model.

\paragraph{The filler floor (\texttt{--pause-len}, and the same probe with filler).} The identical slots filled with content-free tokens, on the same weights. It measures what the extra positions alone are worth---the pause-token effect of \citet{goyal2024think}---and it is the bar a thought must clear to be doing anything as \emph{content}. We recommend it as standard practice: a paper reporting that thinking helps, without this control, has not separated thinking from compute.

\paragraph{The draft-length probe (\texttt{probe\_draft.py}).} The model's own greedy thought truncated to $m \in \{2,4,6,8,12\}$ tokens, against filler of the same length and against the true tokens of the same length, plus the token-level agreement between the two. It converts "our thoughts hurt" into a curve in $m$ and separates a wrong \emph{tail} from a wrong \emph{start}.

\paragraph{The teacher-forced-slot probe (\texttt{probe\_tfslot.py}).} Positional agreement with the true continuation under three conditions on the same anchors: clean text (the ceiling), the true prefix teacher-forced inside the thought slot, and the model's own free-running thought. It splits a broken slot from exposure bias.

\paragraph{The slot-position probe (\texttt{probe\_slotpos.py}).} The same buffer scored twice, changing only the position vector, which tells us what the slot's geometry costs (Section~\ref{sec:slotpos}).

\paragraph{The held-out ruler and the paired benchmark.} Cross-entropy of every checkpoint on disjoint documents from the same three sources, scored by cron during training---the price side of the ledger---and zero-shot likelihood benchmarks scored per example so that thinking and clean modes can be compared on the same weights, and both against the token-matched \emph{and} compute-matched vanilla rungs.

\section{The negative results}
\label{sec:negative}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\linewidth]{figs/fig_hint_value.png}
\caption{\textbf{The reader is not the bottleneck, and our supervised target was outside its reach (N1).} Zero-update, $458$ held-out anchors: a \emph{perfect} $12$-token hint taken from $o$ tokens ahead of the anchor is spliced into the thought slot and the next $64$ true tokens are scored against the same model in silence. After phase A the reader converts a hint about the tokens immediately ahead into $+8.9$ nats and still gains $+4.0$ at $32$ tokens ahead, while a vanilla model that never saw a thought can exploit only the literal next tokens. Value falls to zero near $o \approx 56$ and is \emph{negative} at $o = 64$---the span beyond the scored window, which is what every run from 13 to 23 trained the writer to produce (shaded). The same true tokens shuffled are worth $-0.1$, so the reader needs true \emph{ordered} text, not a bag of words.}
\label{fig:hintvalue}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{N1. The reader is not the bottleneck.} Figure~\ref{fig:hintvalue}. After phase A the model converts a true $12$-token hint into $+8.9$ nats when the hint comes from the tokens immediately ahead, $+6.3$ at $16$ tokens ahead, $+4.0$ at $32$, $+1.5$ at $48$ and $-1.6$ from beyond the scored window; a vanilla model that never saw a thought gets $+8.1$ from the immediate continuation and nothing from anywhere else. The same true tokens \emph{shuffled} are worth $-0.1$. So the reader is strong, order-sensitive, and generalises to hints it was never trained on. Whatever is wrong is on the writing side.

\paragraph{N2. Nothing we can produce at inference beats an empty slot, and by the final design it only ties.} Figure~\ref{fig:belowfiller} and Figure~\ref{fig:ledger}(b). On held-out text, with the model writing its own thought: run 21 $-1.99$ nats, run 24 $-2.45$, run 25 $-1.17$, run 27 $-0.86$, run 30 $-2.25$, run 34 $-1.50$ (greedy; sampled is worse in every case). Content-free filler on the same weights is $-0.74$ (run 25 @900) and $-0.08$ (run 26, trained with filler). The gap is $-0.43 \pm 0.13$ against run 25's own checkpoint, $-0.36 \pm 0.15$ against run 27's, and $-0.64 \pm 0.12$ against run 30 @600's, each on the $n=465$ windows the two conditions share. Only run 30's is paired anchor by anchor ($t=-5.3$, at a greedy $8.6$ tokens against a $10$-token filler); the run-25 and run-27 probes predate the per-anchor dump, so their bars are the conservative independent combination---$3.2$ and $2.4$ standard errors, at $9.4$ and $9.55$ tokens against the same $10$-token filler---and we do not quote a paired bar we cannot compute. The filler floor itself is displaced to $-1.61$ by the scheduled sampling of Section~\ref{sec:run30}. The widest gap of the seven is run 34 @800, the writer that speaks at every position, is $-0.81 \pm 0.08$ below length-matched filler on the same $465$ paired windows, and $-0.69 \pm 0.17$ on the $93$ of them where its own thought is exactly the filler's nine tokens long, so none of it is a length artefact. The ordering holds at every truncation length: at $m=2,4,6,8,12$ tokens run 28 scores own-minus-filler $-0.11, -0.30, -0.51, -0.70, -0.70$. Two other content sources we can generate are worse still: an in-context \emph{recall} span chosen for maximal content-word overlap with the window scores $-3.09$, and a random context span $-2.69$. Seven checkpoints---runs 25, 27, 28, 30, 31, 32 and 34---seven designs, five lengths, one conclusion: the written thought is below the empty slot. \textbf{The scope of that conclusion, measured on the final design rather than assumed.} Every checkpoint just listed is a slide-era design, and no run after 82 passes \texttt{--pause-len} or \texttt{--pause-frac}: the last seventeen runs, the ones that test the reader directly, had never been read against the filler floor. We read two of them, identically. At run 98's final checkpoint (step $5{,}320$, the step the base-damage trigger fired on), at its own deployment anchors---$384$ anchors in $48$ held-out sequences, $\tau > 4$, top-$8$ per sequence, error unit the sequence---the model's own greedy thought and content-free filler of the \emph{same per-anchor length} are indistinguishable: own $-0.221 \pm 0.057$, filler $-0.226 \pm 0.051$, difference $+0.005 \pm 0.017$ nats at $k=1$, three tenths of a standard error, with the own thought ahead at $52\%$ of anchors; at $k=8$ the difference is $-0.009 \pm 0.034$ and at $k=16$ it is $-0.071 \pm 0.035$. On the untrained initialisation every one of these runs starts from, N2's ordering is intact and large: own $-2.757 \pm 0.145$ against matched filler $-2.187 \pm 0.139$, a deficit of $-0.570 \pm 0.139$ ($4.1$ standard errors) widening to $-2.632 \pm 0.301$ at $k=8$. The ordering is therefore a property of the untrained model and of the designs we abandoned, and it does not survive to the end of the programme---not because the thought became useful, but because both arms rose to meet each other. A thought still costs: $-0.22$ nats against silence at run 98's own anchors. What the last design changed is where that cost sits. It is the slot, and the content in it is worth nothing in either direction. One confound we flag rather than decompose: the two checkpoints write different lengths at these anchors ($8.00$ tokens at the initialisation, $6.11$ at run 98, which trained with a six-token budget), so while each own-minus-filler contrast is length-matched by construction, the ${\approx}2$-nat rise in both levels between them is not cleanly attributable to training.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/fig_below_filler.png}
\caption{\textbf{Everything the model can write is below an empty slot (N2).} (a)~Run 29 at step $600$, $465$ held-out anchors: its own greedy thought truncated to $m$ tokens (blue) against content-free filler of the same length (green), both scored against silence on the same anchors. The written thought is below the filler at every length and the gap widens with $m$---the damage is in the tokens it adds. (b)~The paired difference own~$-$~filler for four designs that differ in reward shape and supervision: the calibrated reader (run 25), the near-horizon target (run 29), abstention (run 32) and the return to speaking (run 34). All four are below zero at every length; abstention comes closest by saying less, not by saying anything useful.}
\label{fig:belowfiller}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{N3. It is not the reward shape.} Raw counterfactual gain, shuffle-corrected gain, leave-one-out paired advantages over $R{=}8$ rollouts, and a baseline equal to the model's own greedy thought all produce the same picture. Nor is it the supervision fraction: a quarter, a tenth, and zero were tried; zero collapses into context-free register phrases within a thousand steps (Section~\ref{sec:phaseBres}), and a tenth is indistinguishable from a quarter on every instrument.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/fig_ledger.png}
\caption{\textbf{The ledger: a price paid in every run, a purchase in none.} (a)~Held-out cross-entropy above each run's own starting point, scored on disjoint documents from the same three sources. Run 20 is the learning rate that had never been tested once fp32 parameters unfroze the whole network ($+0.56$ nats in $2{,}300$ steps); at $10^{-5}$ the cost is $0.16$ nats over run 21's $7{,}700$ steps and smaller for the shorter runs plotted ($0.09$ for run 25, $0.02$ for run 29), and it decelerates throughout. (b)~Six later checkpoints measured at inference with \texttt{probe\_thinkmode.py}: the model's own greedy thought (blue) against content-free filler in the identical slots (green), $465$ anchors, paired. Runs 25, 27, 30, 32, 33 and 34 span four reward shapes, an abstention format and a cheap-block reader: the written thought is below the filler floor in every one.}
\label{fig:ledger}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{N4. Reader miscalibration was real, fixable, and not sufficient.} The reader had been trained on a mixture it never meets at inference---a quarter of hints the true future, and every self-written hint the best of eight---and had learned to trust hints. Training it only on one unselected thought of its own (Section~\ref{sec:run25}) halved the cost of thinking, from $-2.45$ to $-1.17$ nats, the largest single move in the project. It then saturated within $600$ steps, and $\mathrm{ob}_0$ was never positive: what was bought is calibration, not content.

\paragraph{N5. The writer can forecast; free running destroys it.} Figure~\ref{fig:freerunning}. With the true prefix teacher-forced into the slot the model predicts the next true token $0.30$ of the time against a clean-text ceiling of $0.41$; writing its own prefix it reaches $0.06$ by the twelfth token, and $0.226$ at the very first token where no prefix exists to drift from. So there are two deficits, of different kinds: the slot itself is handicapped even before any drift, and drift then removes what remains.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\linewidth]{figs/fig_freerunning.png}
\caption{\textbf{The writer can forecast; writing its own prefix destroys it (N5).} Positional agreement with the true continuation on $616$ held-out anchors of one checkpoint (run 31 at step $600$, aligned slot). Grey: the same model predicting clean text, the ceiling. Blue: inside the thought slot with the \emph{true} prefix teacher-forced---close to the ceiling after the first token. Orange: the same slot with the model writing its own prefix---$0.31$ at the first token, where no drift is possible, $0.072$ averaged over positions $1$--$11$, and $0.018$ by the twelfth. Violet: the same free-running measurement before the near-horizon target (run 25), which never left $0.05$. Two deficits of different kinds: the slot itself costs about $0.12$ at position one, and self-conditioning removes most of what is left.}
\label{fig:freerunning}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{N6. The reader pays for a correct thought wherever it functions at all, trained or untrained; what never happens is the model's own thought being one of the thoughts it pays for.} The last third of the programme (runs 82--98, Appendix~\ref{app:history}) stopped asking whether the reader responds to thought content and measured it directly, by splicing text we choose into the slot of an archived checkpoint and scoring the window with no update. Table~\ref{tab:absarms} is that measurement on one fixed intersection of $265$ held-out anchors over $48$ sequences, for four designs and the untrained ladder rung all of them start from: a \emph{verbatim} copy of the true continuation, and a \emph{gold paraphrase}---true, non-verbatim, the target the hint runs were built around. A correct $16$-token thought is worth $+5.3$ to $+9.7$ nats at six of the seven checkpoints, including the untrained one. The reader's willingness to pay is not something any of these designs created, and it is not something any of them destroyed.

\centering
\small
\caption{\textbf{Both absolute arms, at all seven checkpoints (N6).} Per-window gain in nats, clean cross-entropy minus cross-entropy with the thought spliced in; positive means the thought helped. One intersection of $265$ held-out anchors over $48$ sequences, zero updates. The last column is the verbatim arm over all $16$ tokens. Run 95 at step $190$ is the reason this table is printed in full rather than as a contrast: at that checkpoint \emph{any} bracket destroys the window, and the difference between its two arms is the difference between two catastrophes. It recovers to $+7.96$ by step $1{,}140$. The two hint-trained rows sit on checkpoints that have paid base damage, which inflates any gain read off them, so if anything their $+5.3$ is generous.}
\label{tab:absarms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Checkpoint & Design & verb. 1--8 & para. 1--8 & verb. 9--16 & para. 9--16 & verb. 16-tok \\
\midrule

run 94 @$190$ & gate ${+}$ GRPO, no hints & $+12.57$ & $-4.28$ & $-2.84$ & $-0.82$ & $+9.73$ \\

run 95 @$1{,}140$ & best-of-$128$ selection & $+10.93$ & $-6.61$ & $-2.97$ & $-17.68$ & $+7.96$ \\
run 96 @$190$ & $50$--$50$ hint mixture & $+5.54$ & $-0.53$ & $-0.24$ & $-0.11$ & $+5.30$ \\

\end{table}

Two of these designs do move the reader, and in the same direction on both arms. The hint mixture shrinks the verbatim arm from $+12.75$ to $+5.54$ and the paraphrase arm from $-4.53$ to $-0.53$ \emph{within its first $190$ steps}, and the next $5{,}130$ steps of training move neither ($+0.03 \pm 0.26$ on the verbatim arm, pooled over twenty checkpoints of runs 96--98). Run 94---the same bracket format, the same reinforcement, no hint anywhere in its design---is indistinguishable from the untrained model on all five numbers after $3{,}420$ steps, and $190$ ordinary pre-training steps either side of the ladder rung move the verbatim arm by $-0.34$ against the hint run's $-11.21$. So the compression belongs to hint training specifically, not to thinking in pre-training; and because runs 96--98 train on the distribution the probe draws from, we cannot separate \emph{the reader has stopped distinguishing bracket contents} from \emph{the reader is now calibrated to brackets that usually hold roughly-true text}, which is the cheaper explanation. We report the shrinkage and not an interpretation of it.

What the writer does, on the same checkpoints and at the same anchors, is the whole of the negative result. On run 98 the deployment probe scores the model's own $6$-token thought at the $8$ highest-trigger anchors of each sequence, always spoken, and the same thoughts again under a \emph{perfect gate}: spoken only at the anchors where they turn out to help. Over the run's last eight archived rungs ($3{,}990$ to $5{,}320$) the always-spoken arm is worth $-1.49, -1.70, -1.90, -1.70, -1.94, -1.66, -1.80, -1.74$ nats per sequence and the perfectly gated arm $+1.47, +1.32, +1.29, +1.34, +1.58, +1.47, +1.28, +1.52$: about $-0.22$ and $+0.19$ nats per thought, both flat. The gap between them is not a fact about content but a one-sided truncation, and it decomposes exactly: at the final rung $39\%$ of the writer's own thoughts help, by $+0.484$ nats each, and $0.39 \times 0.484 \times 8$ anchors is the whole of the $+1.52$. Read over all $32$ recorded anchors per sequence the two arms are $-6.10$ and $+5.96$, the same picture at four times the volume, with the helping fraction $0.36$--$0.44$ at every rung. This is the one place where the programme's writer is not worthless: two of every five of its thoughts are worth having, and \emph{nothing we can compute without the label finds them}. Of the twenty-one label-free gates in Table~\ref{tab:gates}---the model's own silence token, majority agreement across six samples, reader confidence, confidence gain, thought fluency, anchor entropy, and the two trained gate heads---not one with a speaking rate above $10\%$ reaches zero at $k=1$; the best at a usable volume speaks at $25\%$ and is worth $-0.51$ nats per sequence. Across ninety-eight runs we have never produced a writer whose \emph{unselected} output lands on the first side of that ledger, nor a selector that recovers the part of it that does. A second probe, which asks the writer to do the one thing a self-written thought could still buy---draft the next $8$ tokens greedily and be scored on those same tokens---says the same and locates it: at $384$ high-entropy anchors the trained checkpoint's draft reproduces $2.3\% \pm 0.6\%$ of the tokens it is drafting, against the untrained initialisation's $2.4\% \pm 0.6\%$ on the same anchors. Training moved what the draft costs ($-6.98 \pm 0.34$ nats per thought at the initialisation, $-0.65 \pm 0.11$ at step $4{,}750$, though the two write $8.0$ and $6.1$ tokens so the size of that drop is not attributable to training alone) and left what it says exactly where it was. Clean top-1 accuracy at those anchors is $8.3\%$: the trigger fires where the model does not know what comes next, and in web text that uncertainty is mostly irreducible---names, numbers, markup, section boundaries---rather than computable. A writer cannot draft what no amount of thinking determines, and that, rather than anything about the reader, is where this programme stops.
